3. Move COALESCE outside SUM: score 1/5 (reduces per-row function calls; equivalent).

```sql
with ssr as
  (select s_store_id as store_id,
          sum(ss_ext_sales_price) as sales,
          coalesce(sum(sr_return_amt), 0) as returns,
          sum(ss_net_profit - coalesce(sr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
   from store_sales ss
        left outer join store_returns sr
        on (ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number),
        date_dim d, store s, item i, promotion p
   where ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
     and d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
     and ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
     and ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
     and i.i_current_price > 50
     and ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
     and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
     and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
     and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
     and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
     and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
     and ss.ss_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
     and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
   group by s_store_id),
 csr as
  (select cp_catalog_page_id as catalog_page_id,
          sum(cs_ext_sales_price) as sales,
          coalesce(sum(cr_return_amount), 0) as returns,
          sum(cs_net_profit - coalesce(cr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
   from catalog_sales cs
        left outer join catalog_returns cr
        on (cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk and cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number),
        date_dim d, catalog_page cp, item i, promotion p
   where cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
     and d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
     and cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
     and cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
     and i.i_current_price > 50
     and cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
     and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
     and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
     and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
     and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
     and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
     and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
     and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
   group by cp_catalog_page_id),
 wsr as
  (select web_site_id,
          sum(ws_ext_sales_price) as sales,
          coalesce(sum(wr_return_amt), 0) as returns,
          sum(ws_net_profit - coalesce(wr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
   from web_sales ws
        left outer join web_returns wr
        on (ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk and ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number),
        date_dim d, web_site, item i, promotion p
   where ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
     and d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
     and ws.ws_web_site_sk = web_site_sk
     and ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
     and i.i_current_price > 50
     and ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
     and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
     and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
     and p.p_channel

=== Worker 1: Start Rewrite ===
_radio = 'Y'
     and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
     and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
     and ws.ws_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
     and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
   group by web_site_id)
select channel, id,
       sum(sales) as sales,
       sum(returns) as returns,
       sum(profit) as profit
from (select 'store channel' as channel, 'store' || store_id as id, sales, returns, profit from ssr
      union all
      select 'catalog channel', 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id, sales, returns, profit from csr
      union all
      select 'web channel', 'web_site' || web_site_id, sales, returns, profit from wsr) x
group by rollup (channel, id)
order by channel, id
limit 100;

```sql
WITH ssr AS (
    SELECT
        s.s_store_id AS store_id,
        SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(sr.sr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(ss.ss_net_profit - COALESCE(sr.sr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN store s ON ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
    JOIN item i ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns sr
        ON ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
       AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY s.s_store_id
),
csr AS (
    SELECT
        cp.cp_catalog_page_id AS catalog_page_id,
        SUM(cs.cs_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(cr.cr_return_amount, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(cs.cs_net_profit - COALESCE(cr.cr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_dim d ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN catalog_page cp ON cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
    JOIN item i ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns cr
        ON cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
       AND cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY cp.cp_catalog_page_id
),
wsr AS (
    SELECT
        wsite.web_site_id AS web_site_id,
        SUM(ws.ws_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(wr.wr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(ws.ws_net_profit - COALESCE(wr.wr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM web_sales ws
    JOIN date_dim d ON ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN web_site wsite ON ws.ws_web_site_sk = wsite.web_site_sk
    JOIN item i ON ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN web_returns wr
        ON ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk
       AND ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ws.ws_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY wsite.web_site_id
)
SELECT
    channel,
    id,
    SUM(sales) AS sales,
    SUM(returns) AS returns,
    SUM(profit) AS profit
FROM (
    SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, 'store' || store_id AS id, sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit FROM csr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id AS id, sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
) x
GROUP BY ROLLUP (channel, id)
ORDER BY channel, id
LIMIT 100;
```

</advice>
[
    {
        "group": "Join_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Rewrite comma-style joins into explicit ANSI JOINs; all inner dimension joins (date_dim, store/catalog_page/web_site, item, promotion) are placed before the LEFT JOIN to the returns table. This is semantically equivalent since the outer join only depends on sales columns, but improves readability and join-order clarity."
    },
    {
        "group": "Predicate_simplification",
        "produced_suggestion": "Replace `cast('1998-11-14' as date) + interval '30' day` (which yields a timestamp) with the explicit DATE literal `DATE '1998-12-14'`. This preserves the inclusive range while avoiding timestamp casts and being more index/partition-pruning friendly."
    },
    {
        "group": "Query_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Keep the per-channel CTE + UNION ALL + ROLLUP structure; the final aggregation operates on small aggregated sets (~102 stores + 12000 pages + 42 sites), so no structural change is beneficial. Avoid extra materialized CTEs for date_dim/promotion/item since those are small and the optimizer already pushes filters."
    }
]
</advice>

</analysis>
The query is a TPC-DS style three-channel sales/returns rollup. Main bottlenecks are the large fact tables (store_sales 28.8M, catalog_sales 14.4M, web_sales 7.2M), but the 31-day date filter is highly selective and existing indexes on the date SKs are already exploited; the final ROLLUP/ORDER/LIMIT is negligible. Opportunities were limited to: (1) converting the old comma join syntax with a leading LEFT JOIN into explicit inner joins followed by the LEFT JOIN to returns (safe because the outer join condition references only sales columns), and (2) simplifying the date arithmetic into a plain DATE-to-DATE BETWEEN predicate. Creating extra CTEs for date_dim, item, or promotion was considered and rejected: those tables are small (73k, 102k, 500 rows), the filters are already pushed down, and materialized CTE barriers would only add overhead given the heavy WHERE/JOIN structure. Aggregate expressions were intentionally left unchanged to guarantee exact NULL semantics of `SUM(profit - COALESCE(loss,0))`. The query is already well-optimized; only minor clarity/predicate rewrites were applied.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
WITH ssr AS (
    SELECT
        s.s_store_id AS store_id,
        SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(sr.sr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(ss.ss_net_profit - COALESCE(sr.sr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN store s ON ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
    JOIN item i ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns sr
        ON ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
       AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY s.s_store_id
),
csr AS (
    SELECT
        cp.cp_catalog_page_id AS catalog_page_id,
        SUM(cs.cs_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(cr.cr_return_amount, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(cs.cs_net_profit - COALESCE(cr.cr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_dim d ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN catalog_page cp ON cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
    JOIN item i ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns cr
        ON cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
       AND cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY cp.cp_catalog_page_id
),
wsr AS (
    SELECT
        wsite.web_site_id AS web_site_id,
        SUM(ws.ws_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(wr.wr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(ws.ws_net_profit - COALESCE(wr.wr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM web_sales ws
    JOIN date_dim d ON ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN web_site wsite ON ws.ws_web_site_sk = wsite.web_site_sk
    JOIN item i ON ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN web_returns wr
        ON ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk
       AND ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ws.ws_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY wsite.web_site_id
)
SELECT
    channel,
    id,
    SUM(sales) AS sales,
    SUM(returns) AS returns,
    SUM(profit) AS profit
FROM (
    SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, 'store' || store_id AS id, sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit FROM csr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id AS id, sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
) x
GROUP BY ROLLUP (channel, id)
ORDER BY channel, id
LIMIT 100;

<analysis>
Both candidates apply equivalent rewrites: explicit JOIN syntax, table aliases, and converting the date arithmetic to a literal end date (DATE '1998-12-14', which is correct since '1998-11-14' + 30 days = 1998-12-14). Candidate 1 additionally uses explicit ANSI JOINs with ON clauses for all inner joins rather than the comma/implicit join style, which helps optimizers and readability, and qualifies all column references consistently. Candidate 0 keeps the mixed comma-join style, which can cause ambiguity issues (e.g., unqualified web_site_sk in wsr). Both change returns from sum(coalesce(...)) to coalesce(sum(...)) — note candidate 0 does this while candidate 1 preserves the original semantics exactly (SUM(COALESCE(...,0))), which is semantically identical anyway when NULL rows exist; actually both are equivalent here since sales rows always exist. The key differentiator is the fully ANSI-qualified, explicit join structure of candidate 1, which is the cleaner, safer rewrite.
</analysis>

A careful node-by-node comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs reveals that the query plan structure, operator selection, cost estimates, and row estimates are virtually identical across the original, rewritten, and enhanced versions. The differences that do exist are cosmetic or semantic rather than structural:

1. **Alias / readability changes**: The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce short, meaningful table aliases (ss, sr, s, p, i; cs, cr, cp, p_1, i_1; ws, wr, wsite, p_2, i_2; d, d_1, d_2) replacing fully qualified column references like `store_sales.ss_promo_sk` with `ss.ss_promo_sk`. This improves plan readability but has no execution impact.

2. **Predicate normalization**: In the original, the date filter on `date_dim` is expressed as `d_date <= '1998-12-14 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone`, requiring an implicit cast comparison between date and timestamp. In the rewritten and enhanced versions it becomes a pure date comparison (`d_date <= '1998-12-14'::date`). Although this removes a per-row type coercion, the optimizer already produced the same estimated costs and row counts, so the benefit is theoretical/marginal here.

3. **Join-order reshuffle in the store branch**: In the original, the outermost node of the ssr subplan was a `Nested Loop` joining `store` on top of a `Nested Loop Left Join` (Gather + store_returns). In the rewritten and enhanced versions, this becomes a `Nested Loop Left Join` on top of a `Nested Loop` (Gather + store), i.e., the store lookup is pushed inside the inner nested loop rather than wrapped outside. The costs are identical (1026.07..20048.79 vs 1025.64..20046.59 inner loop with the same total), indicating the planner considers these orders equivalent — essentially a notation/join-order representation change rather than a real refinement.

4. **Identical parallelism, sorting, grouping, and scan strategies**: All three plans use the same Parallel Index Scan / Parallel Seq Scan on `date_dim`, the same Hash Joins against `promotion`, the same index scans on the sales/returns/dimension tables, the same Sort + GroupAggregate structures, the same Append of the three channel subplans, and the same worker counts (2 workers for the store branch, 1 worker each for the catalog and web branches).

Given these observations, the honest conclusion is that the rewrite and enhancement did **not** materially change the physical plan: no scan-type transitions occurred, no join algorithm substitutions occurred, no sorts were eliminated, and no intermediate result sizes changed. All cost figures are unchanged to the cent. Any report claiming large improvements would be unfounded by this evidence; the genuine value of the rewrite lies in readability, predicate typing hygiene, and potentially enabling future optimizations (e.g., if a functional index on the date column were sensitive to the timestamp cast).
</analysis>

</report>

**1. Cost Efficiency**

- **Overall cost change percentage: 0.00%.** The top-level `Limit` cost is 49952.97..49952.98 in all three plans. The rewrite and enhancement produced no measurable change in estimated total cost.
- **Cost change at the most expensive plan node: 0.00%.** The dominant node is the `Nested Loop` inside the store-channel (ssr) subplan under the `Append` (cost ≈ 20048.79), followed by the catalog (≈18780.50) and web (≈11123.22) branches. Every one of these figures is byte-for-byte identical across the original, rewritten, and enhanced plans.

**2. Plan Characteristics**

- **Scan type transitions: none.** All plans retain the same access paths: Parallel Index Scan on `date_dim` (store branch), Parallel Seq Scan on `date_dim` (catalog and web branches), index scans on the three fact tables via their sold-date indexes, primary-key index scans on the returns tables, and index scans on `item`, `store`, `catalog_page`, and `web_site`. No Seq Scan was converted to an Index Scan or vice versa.
- **Join algorithm refinements: none.** Every join remains the same algorithm: Hash Join against the filtered `promotion` table in each branch, Nested Loop joins for the dimension/fact lookups, and Nested Loop Left Joins to the returns tables. The only join-related change is a reordering of the `Nested Loop` / `Nested Loop Left Join` wrapper in the store branch (store lookup moved inside the loop), which carries identical cost and therefore represents no genuine algorithmic refinement.
- **Elimination of explicit sorting / intermediate result reduction: none.** The three per-branch `Sort` nodes feeding the `GroupAggregate` nodes, the `Gather Merge` sorts, and the two top-level sorts all remain. Row estimates (1 row per branch, 3 rows into the final aggregate, 7 rows out) are unchanged, so intermediate result set sizes are identical.

**3. Resource Utilization**

- **Memory usage (Hash/Buffer nodes): unchanged.** All three plans build the same small Hash tables over the `promotion` filter (18 rows, cost 24.25) in each branch. No Hash node was added, removed, resized, or converted to a different strategy.
- **Worker count adjustment: unchanged.** Parallelism is identical in all versions: 2 workers under the `Gather` in the store branch, 1 worker under each `Gather Merge` in the catalog and web branches. No redistribution of parallel workers occurred.

**4. Other Improvements**

- **Predicate typing normalization**: the rewritten and enhanced plans replace the mixed date/timestamp comparison (`d_date <= '1998-12-14 00:00:00'::timestamp`) with a clean date-to-date comparison (`d_date <= '1998-12-14'::date`), removing a per-row implicit cast. This is a good hygiene improvement, though the planner's cost model was insensitive to it here.
- **Readability and maintainability**: consistent, short table aliases (ss, cs, ws, p, i, d, s, cp, wsite, etc.) make the plan substantially easier to read and debug, and reduce the risk of alias-related mistakes in future query maintenance.
- **Join-order clarity in the store branch**: pushing the `store` lookup inside the inner nested loop (below the Left Join wrapper) is a semantically cleaner representation and keeps costs flat; it signals the planner already treats the two orderings as equivalent, so no further manual join-order hinting is warranted.
- **Overall assessment**: the rewrite and enhancement are safe and neutral from a performance standpoint — they neither regress nor improve estimated cost. The plans are already well-formed (index-driven date-range access, selective promotion filters, parallel scanning of the large fact-date joins). Future gains would more likely come from physical tuning (e.g., a date-branched index on `date_dim.d_date` to replace the Parallel Seq Scans in the catalog/web branches, or covering indexes on the fact tables to reduce heap fetches) rather than further SQL rewriting.
</report>

The final union then concatenates strings. But the CTEs already output the string id. If we change CTE to output integer sk and then join dimension after aggregation inside the CTE, we still need to join to get string. The join is after aggregation, so only aggregated rows (number of distinct sk) are joined. This reduces the number of dimension lookups and string grouping. The current plan groups by string, which requires carrying the string from the dimension join to the aggregation. If we group by integer, we can avoid the dimension join until after aggregation. But wait, the join to item and promotion are still before aggregation. The store dimension join can be postponed. For catalog_page, the join to catalog_page can be postponed. For web_site, the join to web_site can be postponed. This is a classic optimization: aggregate by foreign key, then join dimension. Let's design the rewritten SQL.

We need to ensure equivalence. For ssr:
Original:
SELECT s_store_id AS store_id,
       sum(ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
       sum(coalesce(sr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
       sum(ss_net_profit - coalesce(sr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
FROM store_sales
LEFT JOIN store_returns ON ...
JOIN date_dim ON ...
JOIN store ON ss_store_sk = s_store_sk
JOIN item ON ...
JOIN promotion ON ...
WHERE ...
GROUP BY s_store_id

Rewritten:
WITH ssr_agg AS (
  SELECT ss.ss_store_sk,
         SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
         SUM(COALESCE(sr.sr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
         SUM(ss.ss_net_profit - COALESCE(sr.sr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
  FROM store_sales ss
  JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  JOIN item i ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  JOIN promotion p ON ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  LEFT JOIN store_returns sr ON ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
  WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
    AND i.i_current_price > 50
    AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
    AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    AND ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
  GROUP BY ss.ss_store_sk
)
SELECT s.s_store_id AS store_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
FROM ssr_agg a
JOIN store s ON a.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk

But wait: The original query joins store before aggregation. Since store is 1:1 and no

=== Worker 1: Start Rewrite ===
 filter, aggregating by ss_store_sk then joining store is equivalent. However, if there are stores with no sales, they are not included because it's an inner join in original? Actually original has `ss_store_sk = s_store_sk` in WHERE, which makes it an inner join. So stores with no sales are excluded. In rewritten, we aggregate sales by ss_store_sk, then inner join store. Same.

For catalog:
WITH csr_agg AS (
  SELECT cs.cs_catalog_page_sk,
         SUM(cs.cs_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
         SUM(COALESCE(cr.cr_return_amount, 0)) AS returns,
         SUM(cs.cs_net_profit - COALESCE(cr.cr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
  FROM catalog_sales cs
  JOIN date_dim d ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  JOIN item i ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  JOIN promotion p ON cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  LEFT JOIN catalog_returns cr ON cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk AND cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
  WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
    AND i.i_current_price > 50
    AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
    AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
  GROUP BY cs.cs_catalog_page_sk
)
SELECT cp.cp_catalog_page_id AS catalog_page_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
FROM csr_agg a
JOIN catalog_page cp ON a.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk

For web:
WITH wsr_agg AS (
  SELECT ws.ws_web_site_sk,
         SUM(ws.ws_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
         SUM(COALESCE(wr.wr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
         SUM(ws.ws_net_profit - COALESCE(wr.wr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
  FROM web_sales ws
  JOIN date_dim d ON ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  JOIN item i ON ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  JOIN promotion p ON ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  LEFT JOIN web_returns wr ON ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk AND ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
  WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
    AND i.i_current_price > 50
    AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
    AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    AND ws.ws_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
  GROUP BY ws.ws_web_site_sk
)
SELECT wsite.web_site_id AS web_site_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
FROM wsr_agg a
JOIN web_site wsite ON a.ws_web_site_sk = wsite.web_site_sk

Then final union and rollup as before. This rewrite groups by integer SK instead of string ID, and postpones the dimension join until after aggregation. This could reduce the cost of grouping and the number of dimension lookups. The previous rewrite grouped by string ID and joined dimension before aggregation. The optimizer might still choose to push the join down? If we use CTEs, the optimizer in Postgres 12+ will inline them unless `MATERIALIZED`. If we write the aggregation in a subquery and then join, the optimizer might still reorder and join the dimension before aggregation because it's a 1:1 join and no filter. To force the aggregation before the join, we might need to use `MATERIALIZED` CTE or a subquery with `OFFSET 0`. However, the optimizer might recognize that grouping by ss_store_sk and then joining store is equivalent to grouping by s_store_id, and it might choose to do the join before aggregation if it thinks it's cheaper. But if we use a `WITH` CTE with `MATERIALIZED`, it will definitely aggregate first. Is materialization beneficial? It forces the intermediate aggregated result to be materialized. Since the number of groups is small (102 stores, up to 12000 catalog pages, 42 web sites), materializing is cheap. It could prevent the optimizer from joining store before aggregation. But it might also prevent predicate pushdown? The CTE only contains the aggregation, and the join to dimension is outside. The filters are inside the CTE. The optimizer can't push the dimension join into the CTE if materialized. That's what we want. However, materializing the aggregation could add overhead of writing to a temp table. But the result is small, so overhead is negligible. For catalog_page, up to 12000 rows, materializing 12000 rows is fine. It might be faster than grouping by string. Let's consider the plan. If we write:
WITH ssr_agg AS MATERIALIZED (
  SELECT ss_store_sk, SUM(...) ...
  FROM ...
  GROUP BY ss_store_sk
)
SELECT s.s_store_id AS store_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
FROM ssr_agg a JOIN store s ON ...
This forces the aggregation to be a separate step. The aggregation groups by integer. Then joins to store (102 rows) via hash join perhaps. This could be more efficient than the current plan which groups by string after joining store. The current plan's GroupAggregate sorts by s_store_id. Sorting by string is more expensive than sorting by integer. Also, the current plan joins store before aggregation, so it carries the string through the aggregation. The materialized CTE approach aggregates by integer first, then joins. The number of rows after aggregation is at most 102, so joining is trivial. This seems like a solid optimization.

But wait: The original query filters on `ss_store_sk = s_store_sk` and no filters on store. So joining store before aggregation doesn't filter rows. It just adds the string. The aggregation by s_store_id is equivalent to grouping by ss_store_sk. The optimizer could have rewritten it to group by ss_store_sk and join later, but it didn't. Why? Maybe because in Postgres, grouping by a text column is not that expensive for 102 groups, and the join is cheap. But for catalog_page, there are up to 12000 groups, and grouping by string could be more expensive. However, the explain estimates only 1 group. In reality, if there are many groups, the optimization could help. But we need to base on actual data. The data statistics: catalog_page 12000. After filters, how many catalog pages have sales? The date range 30 days, filters on item/promotion/wholesale. Could be many. The explain estimates 1, which is likely because statistics are stale or filters are very selective. But if actual number is large, grouping by string could be costly. So this rewrite is worth proposing.

Let's check if there are any other optimizations. We can also push the aggregation of returns before joining? As discussed, not safe if multiple returns. But we can use `LEFT JOIN LATERAL`? No.

What about the fact that the final query does `sum(sales)` over union all and then rollup. The CTEs already aggregate per store/catalog_page/web_site. The final `sum(sales)` is just summing the pre-aggregated values. That's fine.

We can also combine the three CTEs into a single `UNION ALL` before final aggregation, but that's already done.

So the main new optimization is to aggregate by integer SK before joining the dimension table, and use `MATERIALIZED` CTEs to force it. Let's assign rewrite scores:
- ANSI join and date literal: previous, score maybe 2/10 (cosmetic, no plan change).
- Aggregate by integer SK then join dimension: score 7/10 (reduces string grouping and dimension lookups, could improve performance).
- Avoid materializing small dimension CTEs: no change.
- Keep CTE structure: no change.

We need produce final answer with analysis and rewritten SQL. The user likely expects a single response with the optimized SQL and explanation. Since the previous report said terminate false, we should provide a new rewrite. We need ensure executability, equivalence, efficiency, readability.

Let's write the full rewritten SQL. We'll use explicit ANSI joins, date literals, aggregate by SK in CTEs, then join dimensions, then union all and rollup. We'll use `MATERIALIZED`? Should we use `MATERIALIZED`? In PostgreSQL, `MATERIALIZED` is supported from 12. The explain info shows "Gather" and "Parallel Seq Scan", which suggests PostgreSQL 12+? Actually parallel query exists since 9.6. `MATERIALIZED` CTE is PG12+. The previous rewrite used `WITH` without materialized. The explain shows "Subquery Scan on ssr", which means the CTE was inlined (not materialized). If we want to force aggregation before join, we can use `MATERIALIZED` or we can use a subquery with `OFFSET 0` or `GROUP BY` in a subquery and then join. But the optimizer can still pull up the join. Using `MATERIALIZED` is the most reliable. However, `MATERIALIZED` might prevent the optimizer from pushing the date filter into the CTE? The filter is inside the CTE, so it's fine. It will materialize the aggregated result. Since the aggregated result is small, materialization overhead is low. It might also prevent the optimizer from using an index on store for the join? It will still use a hash join or nested loop on the small materialized set. That's fine.

But is `MATERIALIZED` necessary? If we just write the aggregation in a subquery and join, the optimizer might still choose to join store before aggregation because it's a 1:1 join and no filter. To ensure the optimization, we can use a `MATERIALIZED` CTE. But we must be cautious: `MATERIALIZED` is not supported in all databases (e.g., MySQL, SQL Server). The SQL seems PostgreSQL (EXPLAIN format). The mission likely expects standard SQL? The previous report used `DATE '1998-12-14'`, which is standard. `MATERIALIZED` is PostgreSQL-specific. The mission says "ensuring executability". If the target is PostgreSQL, it's fine. But we can achieve the same by using a subquery with `OFFSET 0` or `GROUP BY` and `HAVING`? Actually, in PostgreSQL, you can prevent flattening by adding `OFFSET 0` to the subquery:
SELECT s.s_store_id, a.sales, ...
FROM (
  SELECT ss_store_sk, SUM(...) ...
  FROM ...
  GROUP BY ss_store_sk
  OFFSET 0
) a
JOIN store s ON ...
This forces the subquery to be materialized (or at least evaluated separately) because of `OFFSET 0`. But it's a hack. `MATERIALIZED` is cleaner. Since the environment is PostgreSQL (EXPLAIN shows Gather, Parallel Index Scan), we can use `MATERIALIZED`. The previous report didn't use it, but we can.

WITH ssr_agg AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT
        ss.ss_store_sk,
        SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(sr.sr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(ss.ss_net_profit - COALESCE(sr.sr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN item i ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns sr
        ON ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
       AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY ss.ss_store_sk
),
ssr AS (
    SELECT s.s_store_id AS store_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
    FROM ssr_agg a
    JOIN store s ON a.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
),
csr_agg AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT
        cs.cs_catalog_page_sk,
        SUM(cs.cs_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(cr.cr_return_amount, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(cs.cs_net_profit - COALESCE(cr.cr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_dim d ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN item i ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns cr
        ON cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
       AND cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY cs.cs_catalog_page_sk
),
csr AS (
    SELECT cp.cp_catalog_page_id AS catalog_page_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
    FROM csr_agg a
    JOIN catalog_page cp ON a.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
),
wsr_agg AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT
        ws.ws_web_site_sk,
        SUM(ws.ws_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
        SUM(COALESCE(wr.wr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
        SUM(ws.ws_net_profit - COALESCE(wr.wr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM web_sales ws
    JOIN date_dim d ON ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN item i ON ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk

       AND ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')

      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ws.ws_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY ws.ws_web_site_sk
),
wsr AS (
    SELECT wsite.web_site_id AS web_site_id, a.sales, a.returns, a.profit
    FROM wsr_agg a
    JOIN web_site wsite ON a.ws_web_site_sk = wsite.web_site_sk
)

    channel,
    id,
    SUM(sales) AS sales,
    SUM(returns) AS returns,
    SUM(profit) AS profit

        "group": "Constant_folding",

    },
    {
        "group": "Predicate_simplification",
        "produced_suggestion": "Kept all selective predicates on item, promotion, date_dim, and fact wholesale_cost in WHERE clauses so they can be pushed down against the large sales tables."
    },
    {
        "group": "Query_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Preserved the three-channel aggregation and final ROLLUP structure. Because each CTE is already aggregated by its channel-specific id, the final UNION ALL plus ROLLUP remains the simplest equivalent form."

</analysis>
The rewritten SQL is structurally equivalent to the original query but improves readability and optimizer friendliness. The implicit comma joins are converted to explicit INNER JOINs, while the sales-to-returns LEFT JOIN is preserved exactly. The date range is folded into a simple inclusive BETWEEN predicate using DATE literals.

Based on the table statistics, the large fact tables are store_sales, catalog_sales, and web_sales, while item and promotion are relatively small filtered dimensions. The current form already applies these filters before aggregation and retains the necessary LEFT JOIN to returns. Pre-aggregating return tables separately is not beneficial here because the return join keys are the sales grain keys and unnecessary CTE expansion could add overhead.

Further structural rewrites, such as replacing the UNION ALL or changing the ROLLUP, would not preserve the same output more efficiently. Therefore, the enhanced SQL is kept the same as the produced

 rewritten SQL.

</produced_sql>

</advice>
[
    {
        "group": "Join_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Convert legacy comma joins with a LEFT JOIN into explicit ANSI inner joins for date_dim/item/promotion, keeping the LEFT JOIN to returns last. This makes join order unambiguous and lets the optimizer apply selective dimension filters before the outer join."
    },
    {
        "group": "Query_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Aggregate on the integer surrogate key (ss_store_sk / cs_catalog_page_sk / ws_web_site_sk) instead of the string display ID, then join store/catalog_page/web_site only after aggregation. This narrows the GROUP BY/sort/hash key to integers, avoids carrying string IDs through aggregation, and reduces dimension lookups to the number of aggregated groups (max 102 / 12000 / 42 rows)."
    },
    {
        "group": "Predicate_simplification",
        "produced_suggestion": "Fold the date expression cast('1998-11-14' as date) + interval '30' day into the constant date '1998-12-14', keeping the comparison on DATE type so the date_dim index remains effective without an implicit timestamp cast."
    },
    {
        "group": "Join_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "The left joins to returns tables are placed after the selective inner dimension joins, so only filtered sales rows participate in the outer join, reducing join cardinality."
    }
]
</advice>

</analysis>
The original plan already pushes dimension filters below the returns outer join, so the previous ANSI-join rewrite produced an identical plan. The remaining improvement opportunity is the GROUP BY key: the plan currently aggregates by string columns (s_store_id, cp_catalog_page_id, web_site_id) after joining the dimension tables. Grouping by the integer surrogate keys and joining the (small) dimension tables after aggregation is equivalent because the joins to store/catalog_page/web_site are 1:1 inner joins with no filters — any unmatched key would be eliminated by the final join exactly as the original inner join did. This reduces sort/hash key width during aggregation and performs dimension lookups only once per group rather than once per fact row. Given table sizes (store_sales 28.8M, catalog_sales 14.4M, web_sales 7.2M vs. store 102, catalog_page 12000, web_site 42), this is the most impactful remaining rewrite. Constant folding of the date predicate keeps the range scan on date_dim index-friendly.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
with ssr as (
    select s.s_store_id as store_id,
           ss.sales as sales,
           ss.returns as returns,
           ss.profit as profit
    from (
        select ss.ss_store_sk,
               sum(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) as sales,
               sum(coalesce(sr.sr_return_amt, 0)) as returns,
               sum(ss.ss_net_profit - coalesce(sr.sr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
        from store_sales ss
        join date_dim d on ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
        join item i on ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
        join promotion p on ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
        left outer join store_returns sr
             on ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
            and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
        where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
          and ss.ss_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
          and i.i_current_price > 50
          and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
          and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
          and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
          and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
          and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
          and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
        group by ss.ss_store_sk
    ) ss
    join store s on ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
),
csr as (
    select cp.cp_catalog_page_id as catalog_page_id,
           cs.sales as sales,
           cs.returns as returns,
           cs.profit as profit
    from (
        select cs.cs_catalog_page_sk,
               sum(cs.cs_ext_sales_price) as sales,
               sum(coalesce(cr.cr_return_amount, 0)) as returns,
               sum(cs.cs_net_profit - coalesce(cr.cr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
        from catalog_sales cs
        join date_dim d on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
        join item i on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
        join promotion p on cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
        left outer join catalog_returns cr
             on cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
            and cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
        where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
          and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
          and i.i_current_price > 50
          and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
          and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
          and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
          and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
          and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
          and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
        group by cs.cs_catalog_page_sk
    ) cs
    join catalog_page cp on cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
),
wsr as (
    select w.web_site_id,
           ws.sales as sales,
           ws.returns as returns,
           ws.profit as profit
    from (
        select ws.ws_web_site_sk,
               sum(ws.ws_ext_sales_price) as sales,
               sum(coalesce(wr.wr_return_amt, 0)) as returns,
               sum(ws.ws_net_profit - coalesce(wr.wr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
        from web_sales ws
        join date_dim d on ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
        join item i on ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
        join promotion p on ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
        left outer join web_returns wr
             on ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk
            and ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
        where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
          and ws.ws_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
          and i.i_current_price > 50
          and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
          and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
          and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
          and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
          and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
          and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
        group by ws.ws_web_site_sk
    ) ws
    join web_site w on ws.ws_web_site_sk = w.web_site_sk
)
select channel,
       id,
       sum(sales) as sales,
       sum(returns) as returns,
       sum(profit) as profit
from (
    select 'store channel' as channel, 'store' || store_id as id, sales, returns, profit from ssr
    union all
    select 'catalog channel' as channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id as id, sales, returns, profit from csr
    union all
    select 'web channel' as channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id as id, sales, returns, profit from wsr
) x
group by rollup (channel, id)
order by channel, id
limit 100;
```

The date arithmetic `DATE '1998-11-14' + INTERVAL '30' DAY` equals `DATE '1998-12-14'` (November has 30 days), so the range is unchanged. All filters (i_current_price > 50, i_category IN ('Books','Home'), promotion channel flags, wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72) are retained identically. -clause predicates on date_dim, item, and promotion turn those comma-joined tables into inner joins, while the LEFT JOIN to returns only affects the returns columns (coalesced). Since the dimension filters are applied to inner-joined rows regardless of join order, and the LEFT JOIN produces NULL return values that are coalesced identically in both versions, the aggregation results are the same. Aggregating on the surrogate key (ss_store_sk etc.) then joining the dimension table afterward preserves group identity because surrogate keys uniquely map to the display IDs (s_store_id, cp_catalog_page_id, web_site_id). The date constant fold (1998-11-14 + 30 days = 1998-12-14) is exact. The final union-all + rollup + order + limit logic is unchanged. Note: the rewritten query assumes a pure date + interval arithmetic where +30 days from Nov 14 lands on Dec 14, which is correct.

    Enhanced SQL 1 is the best choice. It preserves the original query's semantics exactly (same joins, filters, groupings, and rollup) while rewriting the implicit comma-style joins into explicit ANSI JOIN syntax, which improves readability and helps the optimizer in join reordering. It avoids the extra nested subquery/CTE indirection that version 0 adds (joining dimension tables after aggregation), which complicates the plan without guaranteed benefit and risks subtle changes in how the optimizer handles the outer join before aggregation. Version 1 aggregates directly on the fact tables with all dimension joins and filters applied before GROUP BY, matching the original structure closely. The date range is simplified to a literal equivalent ('1998-11-14' to '1998-12-14'), which is semantically identical and more optimizer-friendly. The formatting is also clear and consistent.

## Selected Rewritten SQL: WITH ssr AS (

           SUM(ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,

    JOIN date_dim d
      ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN store s
      ON ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk

      ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk

      ON ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns sr
      ON ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk

SELECT channel,
       id,
       SUM(sales) AS sales,
       SUM(returns) AS returns,
       SUM(profit) AS profit
FROM (
    SELECT 'store channel' AS channel,
           'store' || store_id AS id,
           sales,
           returns,
           profit
    FROM ssr

    UNION ALL

    SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel,
           'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id,
           sales,
           returns,
           profit
    FROM csr

    UNION ALL

    SELECT 'web channel' AS channel,
           'web_site' || web_site_id AS id,
           sales,
           returns,
           profit
    FROM wsr
) x
GROUP BY ROLLUP(channel, id)
ORDER BY channel,
         id
LIMIT 100; ##

Comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs (original, rewritten, and enhanced), the plans are structurally almost identical. The most expensive node in all three versions is the Sort feeding the final Limit, at a total cost of 49952.97, and the dominant Append branch (the store-sales subquery, ssr) costs 20048.80 in every version.

Key observations:

1. Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change percentage: 0% — the total plan cost (49952.97..49952.98) is identical across all three plans. Neither rewriting nor enhancement produced an estimated cost reduction, indicating the optimizer had already converged on a near-optimal plan for this query shape.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: 0% — the topmost Sort/GroupAggregate chain and the heaviest Append branch (ssr, cost ≈ 20048.80) remain unchanged. Minor internal cost shifts occurred only in the store branch of the ssr subquery (e.g., the inner Nested Loop moved from 1025.93..20048.63 / 1026.07..20048.79 in the original to 1025.64..20046.59 / 1026.07..20048.79 after rewriting), but these are negligible rounding-level differences with no impact on the total.

2. Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: none. All access paths are preserved: Parallel Index Scan on date_dim (store branch), Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim (catalog and web branches), Index Scans on the fact tables via sold-date indexes, primary-key lookups on the returns tables, and Seq Scans on the small promotion tables. This is appropriate — the small row estimates (12–18 date rows) make these scan choices already optimal, so no scan transition was needed or performed.
- Join algorithm refinements: essentially none. Hash Joins on promotion keys, Nested Loops for dimension/fact correlation, and Nested Loop Left Joins to the returns tables are retained. The only structural refinement is in the rewritten plan's store branch, where one redundant Nested Loop layer was eliminated: the original joined store after the left join to store_returns, while the rewritten plan joins store inside the inner Nested Loop and applies the left join at a single upper level. This simplifies the join tree (one fewer join operator) without changing the estimated cost.
- Sorting: the outer Sort, inner GroupAggregate input Sort, and the per-branch Sorts (catalog/web branches feeding Gather Merge) are all retained; no explicit sort was eliminated because the GroupAggregate grouping keys still require ordered input and the estimated output rows are too small for the optimizer to invest in alternative strategies.

3. Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: Hash nodes are unchanged (promotion Seq Scan hash builds, cost 24.25, estimated 18 rows in all versions). These are tiny hash tables, so memory pressure is minimal and identical across plans.
- Worker count: unchanged. The store branch uses Gather with 2 workers, and the catalog and web branches use Gather Merge with 1 worker each, in all three plans. Parallelism was already well matched to the small estimated row counts.

4. Other Improvements:
- Readability/maintainability: the rewritten and enhanced plans use short, unambiguous table aliases (ss, p, i, s, sr; cs, p_1, cp, cr; ws, p_2, w, wr) instead of the original's full table names with numeric suffixes (date_dim_1, promotion_2, etc.), making the plans easier to interpret.
- Cast cleanup: the rewritten/enhanced plans compare d_date against a plain date literal ('1998-12-14'::date) instead of the original's timestamp cast ('1998-12-14 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone). This removes an implicit type coercion in the filter, which is cleaner semantically, though the optimizer estimated identical costs either way.
- The enhanced plan is effectively identical to the rewritten plan, indicating that no additional optimization opportunities (index selection, join reordering, parallelism tuning) were available beyond what the rewrite already captured.

In summary, the query was already served by a well-optimized plan; the rewrite delivered structural simplification (one fewer join node in the store branch) and cleaner semantics (aliases, cast elimination), while estimated cost, scan/join strategies, memory footprint, and parallelism remained stable.

Only negligible internal cost shifts (fractions of a cost unit) occurred within the store branch's Nested Loop levels after the rewrite.

2. Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: None. The plans consistently use Parallel Index Scan / Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim, Index Scans on store_sales / catalog_sales / web_sales via sold-date indexes, primary-key Index Scans on the returns tables and dimension tables, and Seq Scans on the small promotion table. These choices were already optimal given the low estimated row counts, so no transition was needed.
- Join algorithm refinements: The join mix (Hash Join on promotion keys, Nested Loops for fact–dimension correlation, Nested Loop Left Join for the returns lookup) is preserved. The one structural improvement from the rewrite is in the store branch: the original plan stacked an extra Nested Loop to join store after the left join to store_returns, whereas the rewritten plan joins store inside the inner Nested Loop and performs the left join at a single upper level — removing one redundant join operator while producing the same result.
- Sorting / intermediate results: All Sort nodes (final Sort, GroupAggregate input Sort, and the per-branch Sorts feeding Gather Merge) remain, since GroupAggregate requires ordered input and the tiny estimated row counts give the optimizer no reason to change strategy. No intermediate result set reduction was achieved; the rewrite's benefit was purely structural, not volumetric.

3. Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: Identical across plans. The only Hash nodes are tiny hash builds over promotion (≈ 18 rows, cost 24.25), so hash memory consumption is negligible and unchanged. Buffer/scan behavior is likewise unchanged since access paths are identical.
- Worker count: Unchanged. The store branch retains Gather with 2 workers; the catalog and web branches retain Gather Merge with 1 worker each. Parallelism was already correctly sized relative to the small estimated cardinalities.

4. Other Improvements:
- Improved plan readability via concise, unambiguous aliases (ss, p, i, s, sr / cs, p_1, cp, cr / ws, p_2, w, wr), replacing numeric-suffixed duplicates (date_dim_1, promotion_2, etc.).
- Cleaner predicate semantics: the date upper-bound filter compares against a pure date literal instead of an implicit timestamp cast, eliminating a hidden type coercion in the filter condition.
- The enhanced plan matches the rewritten plan, confirming there were no further optimization levers (index choice, join order, parallelism) available for this query; the rewrite's value lies in join-tree simplification and maintainability rather than raw cost reduction.

Let's design might have ~100 rewrite rows, store:
- Create might have ~200 filtered rows? dimension CT LetEs: date_filter, item_filter,'s estimate store promo_filter.
- For: store each channel_sales 28.8M, , aggregate31 days -> 28 at the fact+returns.8M / ( level by the5*365) * foreign key to 31 = 28.8M / 182 the channel dimension (5 * 31 = ss157_store_sk,80 * 31 = 489 cs_catalog_page,180_sk, ws_web_site_sk rows. - Then join 3 the aggregated result to.125% -> 1,528 the channel dimension (store, catalog_page; wholesale cost 16% -> 244, web_site) to get the id. So store.
- Then channel might have ~244 rows after filters UNION ALL and final. So store ROLLUP.

This should be more efficient because:
1. Dimension filters (date, item, promotion) are computed once and reused across three branches, reducing repeated scans/filters.
2. The channel dimension joins (store, catalog_page, web_site) happen after aggregation, reducing the number of joins from fact rows to aggregated rows.
3. The per-channel aggregation still happens before final union, preserving channel likely has >100 rows. Thus, the first 100 rows of the final result will be entirely from 'catalog channel' if catalog has >=100 rows, or if catalog has <100, then catalog base rows + catalog subtotal + some store base rows. But store has ~244 rows, so if catalog has <100, the limit 100 will still not reach web channel because the store has enough to small intermediate fill the results.

We should keep it concise but thorough. Desired oververbosity 5. Let's produce final compute the full count from csr. But computing the count from csr requires scanning all catalog sales, which is the same as computing the base rows. Since we are. already computing csr for the base  rows, we can get the count by aggregating csr? Actually csr is already aggregated by catalog_page_id. So the number of rows in csr is the number of catalog pages with sales. We can just count the rows in csr. That count is cheap once csr is computed. So we can create a CTE for csr (the aggregated catalog sales by page). Then we can compute catalog_base as SELECT ... FROM csr ORDER BY id LIMIT 100. We also need the count of rows in csr. We can get that with a separate CTE: catalog_count AS (SELECT count(*) AS cnt FROM csr). Then we can compute catalog_subtotal: SELECT 'catalog channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM csr WHERE (SELECT cnt FROM catalog_count) < 100. This will only produce a row if cnt < 100. Then we union catalog_base and catalog_subtotal. Then we compute store_base only if cnt + (catalog_subtotal count) < 100. We can get the count of catalog_base and catalog_subtotal. Since catalog_base is limited to 100, its count is min(cnt, 100). Actually if cnt >= 100, catalog_base has 100 rows. If cnt < 100, catalog_base has cnt rows. The catalog_subtotal has 1 row if cnt < 100, else 0. So the total catalog rows (base + subtotal) is:
- if cnt >= 100: 100 (since limit 100 on base)
- if cnt < 100: cnt + 1 (if cnt < 100)
We need to know this total to determine how many store rows to take. We can compute a CTE: catalog_total_count AS (SELECT count(*) AS cnt FROM (SELECT * FROM catalog_base UNION ALL SELECT * FROM catalog_subtotal) x). Then store_base = SELECT ... FROM ssr WHERE (SELECT cnt FROM catalog_total_count) < 100 ORDER BY id LIMIT 100 - (SELECT cnt FROM catalog_total_count). Then store_subtotal similar. Then web if needed.

This is doable. But is it necessary to go this far? The user wants a rewritten SQL. We can provide a simpler optimization: remove the web channel because it's not needed due to the limit. But we must ensure equivalence. We can argue that based on data statistics, the web channel is not needed. However, a query rewrite should be equivalent for all data, not just current data. If the data changes, removing web could change results. The mission says "ensuring equivalence". So we cannot just remove web based on data statistics; we need a logically equivalent rewrite. The conditional approach is logically equivalent. So we should implement the conditional approach to be safe.

Let's design a correct and reasonably efficient rewrite.

First, we can keep the three CTEs as they are (ssr, csr, wsr) or we can inline them. But we can also optimize the CTEs themselves. For example, the left join to returns can be moved after aggregation? No, because returns affect sales/profit. But we could pre-aggregate returns by item and ticket/order before joining to sales, to reduce join size? Actually the left join is already indexed. The main cost is scanning sales and joining. We can also push the date filter and other filters earlier. But the explain shows the optimizer already does that. So the main gain is eliminating unnecessary channels.

Let's write the conditional query. We need to be careful with the ROLLUP. The original query uses GROUP BY ROLLUP(channel, id). This produces base rows, channel subtotals, and grand total. We can simulate this with UNION ALL of the components. Since we are limiting to 100, we can compute the components in order and stop when we have 100 rows. The order of components is:
1. catalog base rows (ordered by id)
2. catalog subtotal (if catalog base rows < 100)
3. store base rows (ordered by id)
4. store subtotal (if total so far < 100 and store base rows < remaining)
5. web base rows (ordered by id)
6. web subtotal
7. grand total

But note: The original ROLLUP produces the catalog subtotal after all catalog base rows. If there are >=100 catalog base rows, the limit 100 takes the first 100 base rows, and the subtotal is not included. So the catalog subtotal is only included if the number of catalog base rows is < 100. Similarly for store subtotal: it is included only if the total rows from catalog base + catalog subtotal + store base is < 100. The grand total is included only if all previous rows < 100. Since we are limiting to 100, we can just compute the components conditionally based on the count of rows from previous components.

We can implement this with a series of CTEs that each add rows if the running count is less than 100. But to avoid complex nested subqueries, we can use a single CTE that computes all base rows and subtotals with a sort key, but we add a condition to each CTE to only include rows if the cumulative count up to that point is less than 100. However, the cumulative count depends on the previous CTEs. We can use window functions or subqueries.

Alternatively, we can use a recursive CTE to process channels one by one, but that's overkill.

Given the complexity, perhaps the best answer is to propose the optimization of pushing the LIMIT down and eliminating unnecessary channels, and provide a rewritten SQL that computes all three channels but uses a UNION ALL with LIMIT per branch to allow early termination. However, standard SQL doesn't guarantee that. But we can write a query that uses a CTE with the union of all base rows and subtotals, then orders and limits. The optimizer might not push the limit, but we can manually add a filter: only include rows from channels that could appear in the top 100. Since we know the order of channels, we can add a condition: `WHERE channel <= 'catalog channel' OR (channel = 'store channel' AND ...)`. But we don't know the count

=== Worker 1: Start Rewrite ===
.

Wait, we can use the fact that the final result is limited to 100. We can compute the top 100 by using a nested query with LIMIT 100 on each channel, then union all and limit again. For example:
WITH catalog_top AS (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM csr
  ORDER BY id
  LIMIT 100
),
store_top AS (
  SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, 'store' || store_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM ssr
  ORDER BY id
  LIMIT 100
),
web_top AS (
  SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM wsr
  ORDER BY id
  LIMIT 100
),
all_rows AS (
  SELECT * FROM catalog_top
  UNION ALL
  SELECT * FROM store_top
  UNION ALL
  SELECT * FROM web_top
)
SELECT ... FROM all_rows
ORDER BY channel, id
LIMIT 100;

But this still computes all channels, just limits each to 100. However, computing ssr and wsr to get their top 100 still requires full aggregation of those channels, because the GROUP BY in ssr aggregates all stores. You cannot get the top 100 stores without aggregating all sales. So the LIMIT in the CTE doesn't reduce the aggregation cost. It only reduces the number of rows passed to the final UNION. But the final UNION is cheap. So this doesn't save the expensive part. The expensive part is the aggregation of each channel. To avoid aggregating a channel, we must not execute its CTE at all. That requires conditional execution based on the count from the previous channels. So we need the conditional CTE approach</produced_sql>
```.

This still computes all. To avoid computing store and web, we can use a recursive CTE or a `LATERAL` join to process them sequentially. But a simpler approach: since we know the order, we can write three separate queries with `UNION ALL` and use `LIMIT` in a way that the database can short-circuit. For example:

SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM csr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'catalog channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM csr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'store channel', 'store' || store_id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM ssr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'store channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM ssr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'web channel', 'web_site' || web_site_id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM wsr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'web channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM wsr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT NULL, NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM (
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM csr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
  ) all_sales
) x
ORDER BY channel NULLS LAST, id NULLS LAST
LIMIT 100;

In PostgreSQL, if you have a `UNION ALL` with a `LIMIT` on the outer query, the planner might not push the limit into the `UNION ALL`. However, there is a known optimization: if you use `UNION ALL` and the outer query has `ORDER BY ... LIMIT`, PostgreSQL can use a "Merge Append" for already sorted branches, but it still executes all branches. To force early termination, you can use a `LATERAL` join with `LIMIT`. For example:

WITH catalog_rows AS (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM csr
),
catalog_subtotal AS (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, NULL AS id, SUM(sales) AS sales, SUM(returns) AS returns, SUM(profit) AS profit
  FROM csr
),
catalog_all AS (
  SELECT * FROM catalog_rows
  UNION ALL
  SELECT * FROM catalog_subtotal
),
catalog_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM catalog_all ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100
)
SELECT * FROM catalog_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT * FROM (
    SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, 'store' || store_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
    FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'store channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM ssr
  ) store_all
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
  ORDER BY channel, id
  LIMIT 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top)
) store_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT * FROM (
    SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
    FROM wsr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'web channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM wsr
  ) web_all
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
  ORDER BY channel, id
  LIMIT 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) - (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top)
) web_top
UNION ALL
SELECT NULL, NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit)
FROM (
  SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM csr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
) all_sales
WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM web_top) < 100
ORDER BY channel NULLS LAST, id NULLS LAST
LIMIT 100;

In this query, `store_top` is a subquery that is only evaluated if the `WHERE` condition is true? Actually, the `WHERE` is inside the subquery. The subquery is part of a `UNION ALL`. The planner will still create a plan that includes the `store_top` subquery. But at execution, the `WHERE` condition `(SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100` is evaluated. If false, the subquery returns no rows. However, will it still execute the inner `ssr` CTE to evaluate the `UNION ALL` before the `WHERE`? The `WHERE` is applied after the `UNION ALL`. So the inner `ssr` is still executed. To avoid executing `ssr`, we need to put the condition in a `CASE` or use a `LATERAL` join with `LIMIT 0` when condition is false. For example:

SELECT * FROM catalog_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM LATERAL (
  SELECT * FROM (
    SELECT 'store channel', ... FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT 'store channel', NULL, ... FROM ssr
  ) store_all
  ORDER BY channel, id
  LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100 THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) ELSE 0 END)
) store_top
...

But `LATERAL` with a subquery in `LIMIT` that references `catalog_top` might force execution of `catalog_top` first, and if the limit is 0, the inner query might not be executed at all. In PostgreSQL, `LIMIT 0` returns no rows without executing the query? Actually, the planner might still execute the subquery to get its rows? I think `LIMIT 0` still executes the subquery in PostgreSQL? Not sure. But it's a common technique.

Another optimization: The query uses `cast('1998-11-14' as date)` and `cast('1998-11-14' as date) + interval '30' day`. This can be simplified to `DATE '1998-11-14'` and `DATE '1998-11-14' + INTERVAL '30' DAY`. But that's cosmetic. The previous report did that.
