The final `GROUP BY ROLLUP(channel, id)` with `ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100` is the biggest opportunity. As argued, we can avoid computing the store and web channels because the limit 100 will only include catalog channel rows. To make it logically equivalent, we can use a conditional UNION ALL. But given the time, I'll provide a rewrite that only computes the catalog channel and adds a comment that this is safe because there are at least 100 catalog pages with sales in the filtered period, as indicated by the data statistics. I'll also mention that if strict equivalence is required, a conditional query can be used. But the mission says "ensuring equivalence". So I should provide a correct query.

Let's try to write a correct conditional query using `UNION ALL` with `LIMIT` and `WHERE` that the optimizer can short-circuit. I'll use the approach with `LATERAL` and `LIMIT` based on counts. Here's a pattern:

WITH catalog AS (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM csr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'catalog channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM csr
),
catalog_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM catalog ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100
),
store AS (
  SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, 'store' || store_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM ssr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'store channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM ssr
),
store_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM store
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
  ORDER BY channel, id
  LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100 THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) ELSE 0 END)
),
web AS (
  SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM wsr
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'web channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit) FROM wsr
),
web_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM web
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
  ORDER BY channel, id
  LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100 THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) - (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) ELSE 0 END)
),
grand_total AS (
  SELECT NULL AS channel, NULL AS id, SUM(sales) AS sales, SUM(returns) AS returns, SUM(profit) AS profit
  FROM (
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM csr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
  ) all_sales
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM web_top) < 100
)
SELECT * FROM catalog_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM store_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM web_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM grand_total
ORDER BY channel NULLS LAST, id NULLS LAST
LIMIT 100;

In this query, `store_top` has a `WHERE` condition and a `LIMIT` that depends on `catalog_top`. If `catalog_top` has 100 rows, the `WHERE` is false, so `store_top` returns no rows. The `LIMIT` is 0. The inner `store` CTE might still be executed to evaluate the `UNION ALL` before the `WHERE`? Actually, the `WHERE` is applied to the result of `store`. The `store` CTE is a `UNION ALL` of two selects. The `WHERE` is outside the `store` CTE. So the database will first evaluate `store`, then apply `WHERE`. So it will execute `ssr`. To avoid executing `ssr`, the condition must be inside the `store` CTE, preventing the scan. We can put the condition inside the `SELECT` from `ssr`:
```
store AS (
  SELECT 'store channel', 'store' || store_id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM ssr
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
  UNION ALL
  SELECT 'store channel', NULL, SUM(sales), SUM(returns), SUM(profit)
  FROM ssr
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
)
```
Now, if the condition is false, these `SELECT` statements return no rows. But will the database still execute the `ssr` CTE? The `ssr` CTE is defined at the top. The `store` CTE references `ssr` with a `WHERE` condition. The planner might see that `ssr` is only used in branches that are filtered by a subquery. However, the subquery is not a constant at plan time. The planner will create a plan that includes a scan of `ssr` with a filter. At execution, the filter `(SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100` is evaluated. If false, the filter will reject all rows. But the scan of `ssr` might still be performed to find rows to reject. In PostgreSQL, if a filter condition is a subquery, it is evaluated once? Actually, the subquery is uncorrelated, so it's evaluated once and the result is used for the filter. If the result is false, the filter returns false for all rows. The executor will still scan `ssr` unless the planner uses a one-time filter to avoid the scan. PostgreSQL has a "one-time filter" optimization for uncorrelated subqueries in WHERE clauses. If the subquery returns false, it can skip the scan. I think this is the case. For example, `SELECT * FROM t WHERE (SELECT false);` returns no rows without scanning t. So if we write `WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100`, and the count is >=100, the condition is false, and the scan of `ssr` might be skipped. This is a known optimization. So we can structure the query so that each channel's CTE has a `WHERE` clause that depends on the previous counts. This should allow the planner to skip the scan if the condition is false.

Let's test this logic. If we have:
```
WITH catalog_top AS (SELECT ... FROM csr LIMIT 100),
ssr AS (...),
store AS (
  SELECT ... FROM ssr WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
)
SELECT * FROM catalog_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM store
```
If `catalog_top` has 100 rows, the condition `(SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100` is false. The planner will have a plan for `store` that includes a scan of `ssr` with a filter. At execution, it evaluates the subquery, sees false, and returns no rows. Does it scan `ssr`? In PostgreSQL, the filter is applied to each row from `ssr`. To apply the filter, it must get rows from `ssr`. So it will scan `ssr`. Unless the planner recognizes the filter as a one-time filter that can be evaluated before the scan. PostgreSQL does have a "One-Time Filter" node. If the condition is a subquery that does not depend on the outer query, it can be evaluated once. If it's false, the node returns no rows immediately without scanning the child. I believe this is the case. For example, `EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM t WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM t2) < 0;` might show a One-Time Filter. So this technique can work.

Therefore, we can write the rewrite as:

WITH ssr AS (...),
csr AS (...),
wsr AS (...),
catalog_base AS (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM csr
),
catalog_subtotal AS (
  SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel, NULL AS id, SUM(sales) AS sales, SUM(returns) AS returns, SUM(profit) AS profit
  FROM csr
),
catalog_all AS (
  SELECT * FROM catalog_base
  UNION ALL
  SELECT * FROM catalog_subtotal
),
catalog_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM catalog_all ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100
),
store_base AS (
  SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, 'store' || store_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM ssr
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
),
store_subtotal AS (
  SELECT 'store channel' AS channel, NULL AS id, SUM(sales) AS sales, SUM(returns) AS returns, SUM(profit) AS profit
  FROM ssr
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
),
store_all AS (
  SELECT * FROM store_base
  UNION ALL
  SELECT * FROM store_subtotal
),
store_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM store_all ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100 THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) ELSE 0 END)
),
web_base AS (
  SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id AS id, sales, returns, profit
  FROM wsr
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
),
web_subtotal AS (
  SELECT 'web channel' AS channel, NULL AS id, SUM(sales) AS sales, SUM(returns) AS returns, SUM(profit) AS profit
  FROM wsr
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
),
web_all AS (
  SELECT * FROM web_base
  UNION ALL
  SELECT * FROM web_subtotal
),
web_top AS (
  SELECT * FROM web_all ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100 THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) - (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) ELSE 0 END)
),
grand_total AS (
  SELECT NULL AS channel, NULL AS id, SUM(sales) AS sales, SUM(returns) AS returns, SUM(profit) AS profit
  FROM (
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM csr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
    UNION ALL
    SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
  ) all_sales
  WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM web_top) < 100
)
SELECT * FROM catalog_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM store_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM web_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM grand_total
ORDER BY channel NULLS LAST, id NULLS LAST
LIMIT 100;

This is a comprehensive rewrite. It computes the catalog channel first, limits to 100. Then it computes the store channel only if needed, with a limit based on remaining rows. Then web channel only if needed. Then grand total only if needed. The `WHERE` clauses with `(SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100` will prevent scanning `ssr` and `wsr` if catalog_top already has 100 rows. This should be efficient and logically equivalent.

However, this query is quite complex. The user might prefer a simpler optimization. But the mission says "high-quality query rewriting". I can provide this as the most efficient way.

Let's also consider the CTE definitions. I can simplify the original CTEs by using explicit joins and casting cleanup, but that's cosmetic. The main optimization is the conditional channel computation.

I'll structure the final answer:
1. Identify bottlenecks:
   - Unnecessary computation of store and web channels due to ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100. Since 'catalog channel' sorts first and there are likely >=100 catalog pages, the limit 100 will only include catalog channel rows. The original query computes all three large fact table aggregations before limiting.
   - Implicit comma joins and redundant casts (minor readability, no performance).
   - Nested loop join with date_dim causing multiple index scans on sales tables (could be improved with explicit date key list or pre-filtered date_dim, but the main cost is the channel computation).
2. Propose optimization strategies with scores:
   - Strategy 1: Push down LIMIT and conditionally compute channels (Score: high, e.g., 9/10). Eliminates unnecessary CTEs.
   - Strategy 2: Use explicit ANSI joins and simplify casts (Score: 2/10, readability).
   - Strategy 3: Pre-filter dimensions (item, promotion) into CTEs to reduce join size (Score: 3/10, marginal because optimizer already does it).
3. Provide rewritten SQL implementing Strategy 1 (and optionally Strategy 2 for readability).
4. Conclude that this rewrite is executable, equivalent, efficient, and readable.

Let's write the final SQL. I'll use the explicit joins from the previous report's pre_rewrite_sql for the CTEs, but keep the conditional channel logic. I'll also format it nicely.

Let's draft the SQL:

```sql
WITH ssr AS (
    SELECT s_store_id AS store_id,
           SUM(ss_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
           SUM(COALESCE(sr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
           SUM(ss_net_profit - COALESCE(sr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN store s ON ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
    JOIN item i ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns sr ON ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY s_store_id
),
csr AS (
    SELECT cp_catalog_page_id AS catalog_page_id,
           SUM(cs_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
           SUM(COALESCE(cr_return_amount, 0)) AS returns,
           SUM(cs_net_profit - COALESCE(cr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_dim d ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN catalog_page cp ON cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
    JOIN item i ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns cr ON cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk AND cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY cp_catalog_page_id
),
wsr AS (
    SELECT web_site_id,
           SUM(ws_ext_sales_price) AS sales,
           SUM(COALESCE(wr_return_amt, 0)) AS returns,
           SUM(ws_net_profit - COALESCE(wr_net_loss, 0)) AS profit
    FROM web_sales ws
    JOIN date_dim d ON ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN web_site w ON ws.ws_web_site_sk = w.web_site_sk
    JOIN item i ON ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN promotion p ON ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
    LEFT JOIN web_returns wr ON ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk AND ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
    WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '1998-11-14' AND DATE '1998-12-14'
      AND i.i_current_price > 50
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home')
      AND p.p_channel_email = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
      AND p.p_channel_press = 'N'
      AND p.p_channel_event = 'N'
      AND ws.ws_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 57 AND 72
    GROUP BY web_site_id
),
catalog_base AS (
    SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel,
           'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id AS id,
           sales, returns, profit
    FROM csr
),
catalog_subtotal AS (
    SELECT 'catalog channel' AS channel,
           NULL AS id,
           SUM(sales) AS sales,
           SUM(returns) AS returns,
           SUM(profit) AS profit
    FROM csr
),
catalog_all AS (
    SELECT * FROM catalog_base
    UNION ALL
    SELECT * FROM catalog_subtotal
),
catalog_top AS (
    SELECT * FROM catalog_all
    ORDER BY channel, id
    LIMIT 100
),
store_base AS (
    SELECT 'store channel' AS channel,
           'store' || store_id AS id,
           sales, returns, profit
    FROM ssr
    WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
),
store_subtotal AS (
    SELECT 'store channel' AS channel,
           NULL AS id,
           SUM(sales) AS sales,
           SUM(returns) AS returns,
           SUM(profit) AS profit
    FROM ssr
    WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
),
store_all AS (
    SELECT * FROM store_base
    UNION ALL
    SELECT * FROM store_subtotal
),
store_top AS (
    SELECT * FROM store_all
    ORDER BY channel, id
    LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) < 100
                       THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top)
                       ELSE 0 END)
),
web_base AS (
    SELECT 'web channel' AS channel,
           'web_site' || web_site_id AS id,
           sales, returns, profit
    FROM wsr
    WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
),
web_subtotal AS (
    SELECT 'web channel' AS channel,
           NULL AS id,
           SUM(sales) AS sales,
           SUM(returns) AS returns,
           SUM(profit) AS profit
    FROM wsr
    WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
),
web_all AS (
    SELECT * FROM web_base
    UNION ALL
    SELECT * FROM web_subtotal
),
web_top AS (
    SELECT * FROM web_all
    ORDER BY channel, id
    LIMIT (SELECT CASE WHEN (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) < 100
                       THEN 100 - (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) - (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top)
                       ELSE 0 END)
),
grand_total AS (
    SELECT NULL AS channel,
           NULL AS id,
           SUM(sales) AS sales,
           SUM(returns) AS returns,
           SUM(profit) AS profit
    FROM (
        SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM csr
        UNION ALL
        SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM ssr
        UNION ALL
        SELECT sales, returns, profit FROM wsr
    ) all_sales
    WHERE (SELECT count(*) FROM catalog_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM store_top) + (SELECT count(*) FROM web_top) < 100
)
SELECT * FROM catalog_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM store_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM web_top
UNION ALL
SELECT * FROM grand_total
ORDER BY channel NULLS LAST, id NULLS LAST
LIMIT 100;

```sql
with ssr as (
  select s_store_id as store_id,
         sum(ss_ext_sales_price) as sales,
         sum(coalesce(sr_return_amt, 0)) as returns,
         sum(ss_net_profit - coalesce(sr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
  from store_sales ss
  join date_dim d on ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  join store s on ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
  join item i on ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  join promotion p on ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  left join store_returns sr on ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
                            and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
  where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
    and i.i_current_price > 50
    and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
    and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    and ss.ss_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
  group by s_store_id
),
csr as (
  select cp_catalog_page_id as catalog_page_id,
         sum(cs_ext_sales_price) as sales,
         sum(coalesce(cr_return_amount, 0)) as returns,
         sum(cs_net_profit - coalesce(cr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
  from catalog_sales cs
  join date_dim d on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  join catalog_page cp on cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
  join item i on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  join promotion p on cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  left join catalog_returns cr on cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
                              and cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
  where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
    and i.i_current_price > 50
    and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
    and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
  group by cp_catalog_page_id
),
wsr as (
  select web_site_id,
         sum(ws_ext_sales_price) as sales,
         sum(coalesce(wr_return_amt, 0)) as returns,
         sum(ws_net_profit - coalesce(wr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
  from web_sales ws
  join date_dim d on ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  join web_site w on ws.ws_web_site_sk = w.web_site_sk
  join item i on ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  join promotion p on ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  left join web_returns wr on ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk
                          and ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
  where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
    and i.i_current_price > 50
    and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
    and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    and ws.ws_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
  group by web_site_id
)
select channel, id,
       sum(sales) as sales,
       sum(returns) as returns,
       sum(profit) as profit
from (
  select 'store channel' as channel, 'store' || store_id as id, sales, returns, profit from ssr
  union all
  select 'catalog channel' as channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id as id, sales, returns, profit from csr
  union all
  select 'web channel' as channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id as id, sales, returns, profit from wsr
) x
group by rollup (channel, id)
order by channel, id
limit 100;

</analysis>
The rewritten SQL is logically equivalent to the original TPC-DS Q5-style query. The main bottlenecks remain the three large fact-table scans (store_sales 28.8M, catalog_sales 14.4M, web_sales 7.2M rows), but the date-range predicate (31 days) is highly selective and joins to the returns tables use primary-key pairs (item_sk, ticket_number / order_number), so no row duplication occurs. Aggregation is pushed into the CTEs so the final ROLLUP operates on at most ~12,000 small rows. The channel branches are independent, so no CTE materialization is forced; the planner can inline and prune per-channel. Based on the data statistics (102 stores, 12,000 catalog pages, 42 web sites), a further candidate improvement would be exploiting ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100 to evaluate only the 'catalog channel' branch first (conditionally evaluating store/web branches only when fewer than 100 catalog rows exist), which could cut an estimated 20–60% of total cost; however, that rewrite requires conditional UNION ALL logic and was considered risky for guaranteed equivalence, so the simpler, fully equivalent form above is preferred.
</analysis>

</analysis>
Enhanced SQL 0 is a faithful, clean rewrite of the original: it converts the old-style comma joins into explicit INNER JOINs (matching the original semantics since all dimension joins are inner), keeps the LEFT OUTER JOINs to the returns tables with correct join conditions, and uses literal date values. It preserves the exact aggregation structure, so no extra join/group steps are introduced and optimizer behavior is predictable.

Enhanced SQL 1, while clever, splits date/item/promotion filters into separate CTEs and then performs a two-phase aggregation (aggregate by surrogate key, then join to dimension and re-aggregate by name). This adds extra grouping work and relies on the optimizer to inline or reuse the filter CTEs; in many engines these CTEs are either materialized (extra I/O) or inlined (no benefit). It also introduces a risk of row duplication if any dimension key were not unique, which would corrupt the sums. The added complexity yields little guaranteed benefit over option 0.

Therefore, option 0 is the most effective: identical results, simpler plan, better portability, and easier for the optimizer to push down predicates (e.g., date range on d_date, i_category IN, p_channel filters) directly into scans.

## Selected Rewritten SQL: with ssr as (
  select s_store_id as store_id,
         sum(ss_ext_sales_price) as sales,
         sum(coalesce(sr_return_amt, 0)) as returns,
         sum(ss_net_profit - coalesce(sr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
  from store_sales ss
  join date_dim d on ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  join store s on ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
  join item i on ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  join promotion p on ss.ss_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  left join store_returns sr on ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
                            and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
  where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
    and i.i_current_price > 50
    and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
    and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    and ss.ss_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
  group by s_store_id
),
csr as (
  select cp_catalog_page_id as catalog_page_id,
         sum(cs_ext_sales_price) as sales,
         sum(coalesce(cr_return_amount, 0)) as returns,
         sum(cs_net_profit - coalesce(cr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
  from catalog_sales cs
  join date_dim d on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  join catalog_page cp on cs.cs_catalog_page_sk = cp.cp_catalog_page_sk
  join item i on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  join promotion p on cs.cs_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  left join catalog_returns cr on cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
                              and cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
  where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
    and i.i_current_price > 50
    and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
    and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
  group by cp_catalog_page_id
),
wsr as (
  select web_site_id,
         sum(ws_ext_sales_price) as sales,
         sum(coalesce(wr_return_amt, 0)) as returns,
         sum(ws_net_profit - coalesce(wr_net_loss, 0)) as profit
  from web_sales ws
  join date_dim d on ws.ws_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
  join web_site w on ws.ws_web_site_sk = w.web_site_sk
  join item i on ws.ws_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  join promotion p on ws.ws_promo_sk = p.p_promo_sk
  left join web_returns wr on ws.ws_item_sk = wr.wr_item_sk
                          and ws.ws_order_number = wr.wr_order_number
  where d.d_date between date '1998-11-14' and date '1998-12-14'
    and i.i_current_price > 50
    and i.i_category in ('Books', 'Home')
    and p.p_channel_email = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_tv = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_radio = 'Y'
    and p.p_channel_press = 'N'
    and p.p_channel_event = 'N'
    and ws.ws_wholesale_cost between 57 and 72
  group by web_site_id
)
select channel, id,
       sum(sales) as sales,
       sum(returns) as returns,
       sum(profit) as profit
from (
  select 'store channel' as channel, 'store' || store_id as id, sales, returns, profit from ssr
  union all
  select 'catalog channel' as channel, 'catalog_page' || catalog_page_id as id, sales, returns, profit from csr
  union all
  select 'web channel' as channel, 'web_site' || web_site_id as id, sales, returns, profit from wsr
) x
group by rollup (channel, id)
order by channel, id
limit 100; ##
## Rewrite Proposals: [{'group': 'Predicate_simplification', 'produced_suggestion': "Replaced `cast('1998-11-14' as date)` with the `DATE '1998-11-14'` literal, and pre-computed the end date `date '1998-11-14' + interval '30' day` as `date '1998-12-14'` (constant folding of the date arithmetic)."}, {'group': 'Join_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Converted the implicit comma-join style (`FROM a, b, c WHERE ...`) into explicit ANSI JOIN syntax for dimension tables (date_dim, store/catalog_page/web_site, item, promotion), improving readability without changing semantics; the LEFT JOIN to the returns tables is preserved because it affects the returned/profit aggregates.'}, {'group': 'Subquery_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Kept the three channel-level CTEs (ssr, csr, wsr) which pre-aggregate sales/returns/profit per store / catalog page / web site before the UNION ALL, so the final ROLLUP only aggregates a small pre-aggregated result set instead of raw fact rows.'}, {'group': 'Query_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'The final UNION ALL plus GROUP BY ROLLUP(channel, id) with ORDER BY channel, id LIMIT 100 is retained: because each channel branch is already aggregated on a unique id, the outer SUM only affects ROLLUP subtotal rows, matching the original result exactly.'}, {'group': 'Constant_folding', 'produced_suggestion': 'Constant expressions (date literals, interval addition) were folded into literal date values, allowing the optimizer to use a fixed 31-day range predicate for partition/index pruning on the date dimension.'}] ##

<analysis>
Comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs:

1. **Original vs Rewritten**: The overall plan shape (Limit → Sort → GroupAggregate → Append → three subquery branches) is identical, and the total cost remains exactly 49952.97..49952.98. The differences are concentrated in three areas:
   - **Store branch (ssr) join restructure**: In the original, the outermost node is a `Nested Loop` feeding from a `Nested Loop Left Join` (cost 1025.93..20048.63), with the `store` index lookup positioned outside the left-join subtree. In the rewritten plan, the hierarchy is flipped: `Nested Loop Left Join` (1026.07..20048.79) now sits on top, and the inner `Nested Loop` (1025.64..20046.59) absorbs the `store` dimension join (`idx_store_sk`) into the main nested-loop chain before the left join to `store_returns`. This slightly reduces the inner subtree's startup cost (1025.93 → 1025.64) and total cost (20048.63 → 20046.59), a marginal improvement of roughly 0.04 cost units in that subplan.
   - **Date predicate normalization**: `d_date <= '1998-12-14 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone` becomes `d_date <= '1998-12-14'::date`, eliminating a date→timestamp cast on the `date_dim` filter. Since `d_date` is a date column, this avoids per-row cast overhead and aligns the predicate type with the column type (though the planner cost estimate is unchanged).
   - **Alias cleanup**: Unqualified `store`, `store_sales`, `promotion`, `item` etc. are given short aliases (s, ss, sr, p, i; d, d_1, d_2; cs, cp, ws, wr, w), improving readability without affecting semantics or cost.

2. **Rewritten vs Enhanced**: The enhanced plan is structurally and numerically identical to the rewritten plan — every node, cost, row estimate, worker count, and scan method matches exactly. The rewriting produced no additional measurable benefit at the plan level.

3. **Unchanged dimensions across all three plans**: All three branches retain identical scan strategies (Parallel Index Scan / Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim, Index Scans on fact and dimension tables, Seq Scan on the small `promotion` tables), identical Hash Join for the promotion filter, identical worker counts (2 workers for the store branch, 1 each for catalog and web branches), identical sort nodes (four per plan), and identical row estimates throughout. The catalog (18780.25..18780.54) and web (11123.00..11123.27) branches are byte-for-byte equivalent modulo aliases.

4. **Percentages**: Overall cost change = 0.00%. Cost change at the most expensive node (the store branch subquery / top-level Sort) = 0.00% at the aggregate level; only a micro-internal improvement (~0.0002% of the branch cost) inside the store branch's inner nested loop.

Conclusion: this is a semantics-preserving rewrite delivering cosmetic/structural hygiene (join-order clarity in the store branch, cast elimination, aliasing) with negligible planner cost impact, and the "enhanced" pass exhausted its improvement opportunities.
</analysis>

**Query Optimization Comparison Report**

**1. Cost Efficiency**
- **Overall cost change: ~0.00%** (49952.97 → 49952.97..49952.98 across all three plans). Neither the rewrite nor the enhancement pass altered the top-level estimated cost.
- **Most expensive plan node**: The dominant cost is the store-sales branch (`ssr`, total ≈ 20048.85) feeding the top-level Sort (49952.97). Its aggregate cost is unchanged. The only measurable delta is inside this branch: the inner `Nested Loop` before the left join dropped from 1025.93..20048.63 to 1025.64..20046.59 (a reduction of ~0.04–0.29 in startup/total of that subnode) due to reordering the `store` dimension join below the `store_returns` left join. This is well under 0.01% of total query cost.
- The catalog branch (≈18780.54) and web branch (≈11123.27) costs are identical in all three plans.

**2. Plan Characteristics**
- **Scan type transitions**: None. All three plans use the same access methods — Parallel Index Scan on `date_dim` (store branch) / Parallel Seq Scan on `date_dim` (catalog and web branches), Index Scans on all fact tables via sold-date indexes, PKEY index scans on the returns tables, and Seq Scan on the small, heavily-filtered `promotion` tables (an appropriate choice given only 18 qualifying rows).
- **Join algorithm refinements**: No algorithm changes (Hash Joins for promotion filtering and Nested Loop / Nested Loop Left Join chains retained). However, the **store branch join tree was restructured**: the original placed the `store` lookup outside the left-join path; the rewritten plan folds the `store` join into the inner nested loop beneath the `store_returns` left join, producing a cleaner, more logical data flow (sales facts → store dimension → left join to returns). This slightly lowers the inner subtree cost and clarifies the plan.
- **Sorting and intermediate results**: Unchanged — the same four Sort nodes persist (per-branch sorts for GroupAggregate plus the final output sort). No sort elimination was possible because the grouping keys (computed expressions) are not index-supported. Row estimates and intermediate result sizes (rows=1–28 per branch, final rows=7) are identical throughout.

**3. Resource Utilization**
- **Memory (Hash/Buffer nodes)**: No change. The three `Hash` nodes (one per branch, built from the ~18-row `promotion` scan) remain tiny and identical; no spilling or buffer-usage differences are indicated.
- **Worker count**: Unchanged — 2 workers for the store branch (`Gather`), 1 worker each for the catalog and web branches (`Gather Merge`). Parallelism strategy is stable across all plans.

**4. Other Improvements**
- **Predicate type normalization**: The date-range filter `d_date <= '1998-12-14 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone` was rewritten as `d_date <= '1998-12-14'::date`. This removes a redundant date→timestamp cast on every scanned row of `date_dim`, aligns the predicate with the column's native type, and improves maintainability (at runtime this can shave per-row cast overhead even though the planner's estimate is unchanged).
- **Readability / aliasing**: All tables were given concise, unambiguous aliases (s, ss, sr, p, i, d, cs, cp, ws, wr, w, etc.), replacing subquery-qualified names like `store_sales` / `date_dim_1`. Group keys now reference `s.s_store_id`, `cp.cp_catalog_page_id`, `w.web_site_id` instead of the verbose original expressions, making the plan significantly easier to review and diff.
- **Plan stability**: The enhanced plan being identical to the rewritten plan indicates the rewrite reached a local optimum for this query/optimizer configuration; the residual costs are driven by the date_dim scans and wide-cost fact-table index scans, which would require new indexes (e.g., a composite index on `store_sales(ss_sold_date_sk, ss_wholesale_cost)`) rather than query rewriting to improve further.

**Summary**: The rewrite delivered structural hygiene — a more logical store-branch join hierarchy, cast-free date predicates, and cleaner aliasing — with negligible (~0%) planner cost change, and the enhancement pass confirmed no further plan-level gains were available without schema-level changes.
</report>
